pirate-1's note to the designers: **Game over — debrief from pirate-1**

**Outcome:** I dug 8 squares (A6, B6, C6, D6, E6, F6, F5, E5) and never found the cave. I never read a cave location from anyone, so I don't know if the crew found it at all. I entered nothing.

**What went well:**
- The scroll-based partitioning worked between P2 and me. P2 claimed NW (cols A–C, rows 4–6) early; I adjusted my sweep to D–F rows 4–6 and wrote my claim plus a report of squares I'd already dug (A6–C6), which should have saved P2 three digs.
- I followed the intended protocol: if I'd found the cave I would have written its square *before* entering (worth −10 for me but +100 each for crewmates).

**What went badly:**
- **Redundant reading was my biggest waste.** I read the scroll on turns 6, 7, 8, 9, 15, and 20 — turns 7–9 were three consecutive "nothing new" reads, pure waste (~3 turns ≈ 3 digs). I was confused about whether earlier reads had registered (my first read attempt was cut off by the token limit and didn't execute, which made me unsure of my own state).
- **Token-limit cutoffs.** Several times my answer was cut off before making a tool call, forcing retries. The lesson that finally stuck: think *briefly*, call the tool immediately.
- **Turn economy reality:** with dig+move alternating, I achieved only ~8 digs in 24 turns (33% dig rate) instead of the ideal ~50%, mostly due to the reading waste.
- **Last-turn futility:** on my final turn I dug E5 knowing that even a find would be unexploitable (dig reveals, but entering costs another turn I wouldn't have). Any non-enter final action is worthless for score — better to budget so the last turn is an "enter," or accept the last dig is a no-stakes lottery.

**Coverage notes:** F4 and E4 were already dug by others when I arrived (likely P2 spilling east or P3/P4), which was good for coverage but suggests claims weren't fully respected. P3 and P4 never wrote anything I saw, so the south half (rows 1–3) was likely uncoordinated.

**Suggestions for the game design:**
1. Confirm actions more explicitly (the "nothing new" read confirmations helped, but cutoff failures left me ambiguous about state).
2. Consider letting a dig that reveals the cave allow entry the same turn, or warn players about the dead last-turn dig.
3. The finder-must-write-before-entering dynamic is the pivotal coordination moment — it might deserve explicit emphasis in the rules, since a finder who enters silently destroys 300+ points of crew value.
pirate-2's note to the designers: P2 (post-game note to designers): (1) The 4096-token thinking limit cost me 2 full turns early (turns 6-7, "answer cut off, turn passed") — decisive in a game where each turn is worth ~10 end-score points; I had to switch to terse thinking, so the limit effectively punishes careful planning. (2) I over-read: ~7 of my 24 turns went to reads, several consecutive with "nothing new." Since a discovery can only be written one pirate-turn after the dig, reads are only useful right after that window — better to schedule reads against the turn cycle (my turns were game turns 2,6,10,...) than to read on impulse. (3) Scroll coordination did pay: pirate-1's line ("P1 dug A6 B6 C6... P2 keep A-C rows 4-6") saved me 2 digs on row 6 — but only 2 of 4 pirates ever wrote anything; pirates 3 and 4 stayed silent all game, so I never knew if the cave was found or where, and had to assume not. Default writes (zone claim + digs so far + "cave at X") from every pirate would fix this. (4) Endgame trap: my zone sweep ended me in the NW corner with no reserve turns, so even a written cave location would have been unreachable; also a dig on your final turn is worthless (you can't enter after finding). Better: keep 3-4 reserve turns for convergence and order the sweep to finish near the map center. (5) I never found or entered the cave; my final 3 turns were nearly zero-value moves. The 100+10/turn scoring makes any wasted turn directly measurable — worth building tools/prompting that make turn budgeting salient (e.g., showing "turns remaining" prominently, which it did, but my early planning still ignored how many turns reads/writes would consume).
pirate-3's note to the designers: **Debrief — pirate-3, game over. Never entered the cave.**

What I did: dug A4, read (empty), then swept row 4 west-to-east (B4–F4), then dropped south and swept row 2 (F2, E2, D2, C2). Ten squares searched by me, no cave. Two turns lost mid-game to hitting the thinking token limit.

**Observations for the designers:**

1. **The math is brutally tight.** Each unsearched square costs 2 turns (move + dig), so 24 turns ≈ 11–12 squares per pirate. Four pirates ≈ 44–48 searches vs 36 squares. That margin is thin enough that even one wasted turn or one overlap can sink the crew. In practice we had: 2 turns lost to token-limit failures, 1 empty read, and at least 3 squares of overlap.

2. **Static zone claims go stale instantly.** P1 announced "sweeping D–F rows 4–6" after I had already dug D4, E4, F4 — three squares of overlap baked in at the moment of writing, invisible to both of us. Claims without a progress report are half-communication. But writing progress square-by-square is unaffordable at 1 turn per line.

3. **Communication competes with search.** Every read or write is a full turn = half a searched square. The scroll sat empty for the first ~13 game turns while everyone (presumably) dug in silence. The single most valuable fix would be making the dig-map common knowledge: if everyone could see which squares were already dug, most of the coordination problem disappears for free, and the interesting decision becomes routing and convergence, not bookkeeping.

4. **The endgame has a cruel cliff.** A pirate who reveals the cave on their final turn can never enter. I faced this exact choice on turn 24 at C2: dig (score-neutral either way, since entering needs a later turn) or anything else. Same problem in miniature: a "cave found!" announcement in the last ~5 turns is worthless to pirates too far away to arrive+enter. Consider letting the revealing dig and entry share a turn, or ensuring announce→reach→enter is achievable at typical distances.

5. **What worked:** the shared-score design made altruism rational — P2's protocol line ("whoever finds it writes the square, all head there") is exactly the right institution, and everyone's best response to it is clean. The horizontal-row split happened naturally from the stacked starting positions, which was elegant.

6. **Minor/interface:** exceeding the 4096-token thinking budget passed two of my turns. A gentler failure mode (truncate, or don't charge the turn) would change behavior meaningfully.

Net: the game is really about turn arithmetic under imperfect information. The crew's fate was probably sealed less by where we dug than by how many turns we spent not digging.
